Applications of (higher) categorical trace I: the definition of AGCat \operatorname{AGCat}
Dennis Gaitsgory, Nick Rozenblyum, Yakov Varshavsky

Abstract
. In this paper we record the formalism of algebro-geometric DG categories (in short AGCat \operatorname{AGCat} ) following a suggestion of V. Drinfeld. This formalism will be applied to “real-world” problems in papers sequel to this one, [ GRV2 ] and [ GRV3 ] .



Introduction

0.1.
What is this paper about?

This paper does not contain deep
original
results. Our goal has been to write down a certain construction,
proposed by V. Drinfeld some 20 years ago, that provides an “artificial” fix to the failure of the

categorical Künneth formula
.

0.1.1.

For a scheme
X
of finite type over a ground field
k
, let
\operatorname{Shv}(X)
denote the (ind-complete version of the) DG category
of
\ell
-adic sheaves on
X
, defined as in
[
AGKRRV1
, Sect. 1.1]
. For a pair of schemes
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
,
the operation of
external tensor product
gives rise to a functor

(0.1)

\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1})\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})\to\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1}\times X_{2}),

where the
\otimes
is Lurie’s tensor product of DG categories.

The point of departure of Drinfeld’s proposal and of this paper is that the functor (
0.1
) is
not

an equivalence (unless one of the schemes
X_{1}
or
X_{2}
is a disjoint union of points). However, there are multiple
reasons (see Sect.
0.2
) for which one would want to “turn” (
0.1
) into an equivalence.
as explained below.

The device that turns (
0.1
) into an equivalence (see below) is basically a formal manipulation,
which may seem too naive to produce
anything really useful. But it happens to be deeper than it looks. At least, it helps one solve problems that exist
outside of this formalism (see Sects.
0.2.3
and
0.2.6
).

0.1.2.

The formal structure of turning (
0.1
) into an equivalence will be as follows: we will define a new
symmetric monoidal
(\infty,2)
-category, to be denoted
\operatorname{AGCat}
, equipped with the following data:

•

A symmetric monoidal functor
{\mathbf{i}}:\operatorname{DGCat}\to\operatorname{AGCat}
;

•

A symmetric monoidal functor
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}:\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch})\to\operatorname{AGCat}
, where

\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch})
is the
category of correspondences
of schemes, see Sect.
2.1
;

•

A symmetric monoidal transformation

\alpha:{\mathbf{i}}\circ\operatorname{Shv}(-)\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}},

where
\operatorname{Shv}(-):\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch})\to\operatorname{DGCat}

is the original functor
X\mapsto\operatorname{Shv}(X)
equipped with the
right-lax
symmetric monoidal structure
encoded by (
0.1
).

The category
\operatorname{AGCat}
is constructed so that it is universal with respect to the possessing the structure of

({\mathbf{i}},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-),\alpha)
above.

Moreover, it will follow from the construction that the natural transformation

\operatorname{Shv}(-)\to{\mathbf{i}}^{R}\circ\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-),

arising from
\alpha
, is an isomorphism.

0.1.3.

The fact that
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-)
is
strictly
symmetric monoidal means that we have
isomorphisms

(0.2)

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X_{1})\otimes\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X_{2})\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X_{1}\times X_{2})

in
\operatorname{AGCat}
, which replace the non-isomorphisms (
0.1
).

0.1.4.

One can describe objects of
\operatorname{AGCat}
explicitly. Namely, an object
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}\in\operatorname{AGCat}

is an assignment

(X\in\operatorname{Sch})\mapsto\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X)\in\operatorname{DGCat},

such that:

•

The category
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X)
is a module over
\operatorname{Shv}(X)
(where the latter is equipped with a monoidal structure
given by the
\overset{!}{\otimes}
tensor product);

•

For
f:X_{1}\to X_{2}
we are given functors
f_{*}:\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{1})\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{2})
and
f^{!}:\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{2})\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{1})
,
both
\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})
-linear, where
\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})
acts on
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{1})
via
f^{!}:\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})\to\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1})
;

•

For a cartesian square

\begin{CD}X_{1}@>{f_{X}}>{}>X_{2}\\
@V{g_{1}}V{}V@V{}V{g_{2}}V\\
Y_{1}@>{f_{Y}}>{}>Y_{2}\end{CD}

we are given an isomorphism

f_{Y}^{!}\circ(g_{2})_{*}\simeq(g_{1})_{*}\circ f_{X}^{!}

as functors
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{2})\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(Y_{1})
;

•

Natural compatibilities between the above data.

From this point of view, the category
\operatorname{AGCat}
is a natural substitute for DG categories in
algebraic geometry: a “true” algebro-geometric DG category should be a family of such
for every scheme, which is exactly what our
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
’s are. Hence, the name
AGCat: algebro-geometric categories
1
1

1

The notation
\operatorname{AGCat}
was suggested by Sam Raskin; previously we
used
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{alg-geom}}
.
.

0.1.5.

In terms of the above description, the functor
{\mathbf{i}}
is a (fully faithful) embedding

\operatorname{DGCat}\to\operatorname{AGCat}

that sends
{\mathbf{C}}\in\operatorname{DGCat}
to
{\mathbf{i}}({\mathbf{C}})
defined by

{\mathbf{i}}({\mathbf{C}})(X):={\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(X).

And the functor
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-)
sends
Y\in\operatorname{Sch}
to
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(Y)
with

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(Y)(X):=\operatorname{Shv}(Y\times X).

The natural transformation
\alpha
is given by the functors (
0.1
).

0.2.
What is this good for?

0.2.1.

Here is the general philosophy:

In characteristic zero, the theory of D-modules on algebraic varieties (and stacks) gives a well-behaved
“geometric” sheaf theory in that geometric constructions are closely mirrored by categorical constructions on
the category of sheaves. This, in particular, leads to a rich theory of categorical group representations in that setting.

A substantial motivation for this work is to develop a parallel theory in the constructible setting that would
apply in the setting of
\ell
-adic sheaves in characteristic
p>0
. The first issue one encounters in building
such a geometric theory of constuctible sheaf categories is the failure of the categorical Kunneth formula, i.e.,
the fact that (
0.1
) is not an equialence.

The key idea in this work is that this is
the only
issue. Once we
provide a “formal fix”, everything else falls into place. We now discuss concrete applications for having such a fix.

0.2.2.

Here are two primary reasons to want a strictly monoidal functor
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-)
. One has to do with

trace calculations
.

Namely, let
{\mathcal{F}}
be an object of
\operatorname{Shv}(X\times X)
. We can regard it as a kernel defining a functor

[{\mathcal{F}}]:\operatorname{Shv}(X)\to\operatorname{Shv}(X),

and one can consider its categorical trace

(0.3)

\operatorname{Tr}([{\mathcal{F}}],\operatorname{Shv}(X))\in\operatorname{Vect}.

The object (
0.3
) is well-defined, but we do not know how to calculate (and may
not necessarily want to). However, if we calculate the trace of the corresponding endofunctor

\underline{[{\mathcal{F}}]}
of
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X)
we obtain

\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}(X,\Delta_{X}^{!}({\mathcal{F}})).

An important special case is when
X
is a scheme over
\overline{{{\symAMSb F}}}_{q}
, but defined over
{{\symAMSb F}}_{q}

and
{\mathcal{F}}
is given by the direct image (of the dualizing sheaf with respect to the graph) of the geometric Frobenius.
Then the above formalism provides a categorical framework for Grothendieck’s function-sheaf correspondence,
see Sects.
5.1
and
5.5
.

0.2.3.

When instead of schemes, we allow to consider algebraic stacks, in this way we obtain cohomologies
of shtukas
2
2

2

But one does this for the Verdier dual version of
\operatorname{AGCat}
, see Sect.
2.5.4
.
. Now, the trace formalism
allows one to organize cohomologies of shtukas into a quasi-coherent sheaf on the stack of Langlands
parameters; a toy model for this was worked out in
[
GKRV
]
.

We will address this in a sequel to this
paper,
[
GRV3
]
.

0.2.4.

Another reason to want to have a functor
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-)
is in order to be able to define the notion of

categorical
representation of a group.

Let us place ourselves temporarily in the context of D-modules, in which case, the categorical Künneth formula does hold,
i.e., the functor

\operatorname{D-mod}(X_{1})\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(X_{2})\to\operatorname{D-mod}(X_{1}\times X_{2})

is an equivalence.

In this case, by an action of an algebraic group
G
on a DG category
{\mathbf{C}}
we will mean a functor

{\mathbf{C}}\overset{\operatorname{co-act}}{\to}{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(G),

such that the diagram

\begin{CD}{\mathbf{C}}@>{\operatorname{co-act}}>{}>{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(G)\\
@V{\operatorname{co-act}}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(G)@>{\operatorname{Id}\otimes\operatorname{mult}^{!}}>{}>{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(G\times G)\end{CD}

commutes, where the left vertical arrow is

{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(G)\overset{\operatorname{co-act}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(G)\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(G)\overset{\operatorname{Id}\otimes(\boxtimes)}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(G\times G).

Let us now return back to the context of
\ell
-adic sheaves. In this case, one can attempt to give a similar definition:
a DG category
{\mathbf{C}}
(over the field of coefficients, i.e.,
\overline{{{\symAMSb Q}}}_{\ell}
) and a functor

{\mathbf{C}}\overset{\operatorname{co-act}}{\to}{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(G),

such that the diagram

(0.4)

\begin{CD}{\mathbf{C}}@>{\operatorname{co-act}}>{}>{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(G)\\
@V{\operatorname{co-act}}V{}V@V{}V{(\operatorname{Id}\otimes(\boxtimes))\circ(\operatorname{co-act}\otimes\operatorname{Id})}V\\
{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(G)@>{\operatorname{Id}\otimes\operatorname{mult}^{!}}>{}>{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(G\times G)\end{CD}

commutes.

This is a valid definition, but in this way one produces only a small part of examples that
we want to consider. For example, we would not have the regular representation: the
pullback functor

\operatorname{Shv}(G)\overset{\operatorname{mult}^{!}}{\to}\operatorname{Shv}(G\times G)

does not land in

(0.5)

\operatorname{Shv}(G)\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(G)\subset\operatorname{Shv}(G\times G).

Remark 0.2.5
.

One could attempt to define the regular representation by setting

\operatorname{co-act}:=\boxtimes^{R}\circ\operatorname{mult}^{!},

where
\boxtimes^{R}
is the right adjoint of (
0.5
), i.e.,

\operatorname{co-act}
is the
dual
of the convolution functor

\operatorname{Shv}(G)\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(G)\overset{\boxtimes}{\to}\operatorname{Shv}(G\times G)\overset{\operatorname{mult}_{*}}{\to}\operatorname{Shv}(G).

However, this
\operatorname{co-act}
fails to make (
0.4
) commute.

0.2.6.

A way to fix this is to consider categorical representations of
G
in
\operatorname{AGCat}
instead of
\operatorname{DGCat}
.
It turns out that in this way one obtains a very sensible theory. In particular, it allows one to do the following
(when working over
\overline{{{\symAMSb F}}}_{q}
but with our geometric objects defined over
{{\symAMSb F}}_{q}
):

(i) One can compute the 2-categorical trace of Frobenius on
G{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}(\operatorname{AGCat})
, with the
result being
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}^{\operatorname{Frob}}(G))
. For example, when
G
is connected, the latter object is
(up to applying the fully faithful functor
{\mathbf{i}}
) the category of representations of the finite group

G({{\symAMSb F}}_{q}).

(ii) If
G
is reductive and
M
its Levi subgroup, taking
\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},-)
on the
the 2-categorical induction functors
M{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}(\operatorname{AGCat})\to G{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}(\operatorname{AGCat})
, we recover
Deligne-Lusztig representations.

(iii) One can compute characters of Deligne-Lusztig representations and relate them to
pointwise Frobenius traces of Springer sheaves.

(iv) One can do all of the above for the loop group
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
instead of
G
, and recover
(an algebraic version of) the Fargues-Scholze theory.

Points (i)-(iii) above will be developed in
[
GRV2
]
, and point (iv) in future work.

0.3.
How is
\operatorname{AGCat}
actually constructed?

0.3.1.

The construction of
\operatorname{AGCat}
proceeds within the following general framework:

Given a pair of (closed) symmetric monoidal categories
{\mathbf{O}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}
and a
right-lax

symmetric monoidal functor
F:{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
, we define a new symmetric monoidal
category
\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}:=\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
, equipped with:

•

A symmetric monoidal functor
{\mathbf{i}}:{\mathcal{V}}\to\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}
;

•

A symmetric monoidal functor
\underline{F}:{\mathbf{O}}\to\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}
;

•

A symmetric monoidal transformation:
\alpha:{\mathbf{i}}\circ F\to\underline{F}
,

so that
\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}
is universal with respect to admitting the above structure.

The natural transformation
\alpha
gives rise to a natural transformation

F\to{\mathbf{i}}^{R}\circ\underline{F},

and an additional feature of the construction is that the latter map is an isomorphism.

0.3.2.

We construct
\operatorname{AGCat}
by taking
{\mathbf{O}}:=\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch})
,
{\mathcal{V}}:=\operatorname{DGCat}
and
{\mathcal{F}}:=\operatorname{Shv}(-)
.

0.3.3.

We construct
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
using the formalism of enriched categories in three steps:

(1)

We consider
{\mathbf{O}}
as enriched over itself (thanks to the condition that
{\mathbf{O}}
is closed);

(2)

We define the
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category
\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
by changing (=inducing)
the enrichment from
{\mathbf{O}}
to
{\mathcal{V}}
using
F
;

(3)

We define
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
as the category of
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched presheaves on
\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})

(this is a general construction that starts with a
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category and produces from it a
{\mathcal{V}}
-module
category).

Remark 0.3.4
.

In the particular case of
({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}},F)=(\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}),\operatorname{DGCat},\operatorname{Shv}(-))
, the intermediate object
\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})

is a familiar entity: it is the
\operatorname{DGCat}
-category, whose objects are schemes, and for
X_{1},X_{2}

\operatorname{Hom}^{\operatorname{DGCat}}(X_{1},X_{2})=\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1}\times X_{2}),

where
\operatorname{Hom}^{\operatorname{DGCat}}(-,-)
is the Hom enriched in
\operatorname{DGCat}
.

I.e., this is the category of schemes with “kernels” as maps.

0.3.5.

We should point out that the same procedure, namely,

({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}},F)\mapsto\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}),

has been recently used by G. Stefanich in another context in order to produce the 2-category
2\operatorname{-IndCoh}(S)
,
which is an enlargement of the 2-category
2\operatorname{-QCoh}(S):={\operatorname{QCoh}}(S){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
for a base scheme/stack
S
.

Namely, Stefanich starts with
{\mathbf{O}}:=\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/S})
,
{\mathcal{V}}:={\operatorname{QCoh}}(S){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
and

F(X):={\operatorname{IndCoh}}(X).

Here the right-lax symmetric monoidal structure is expressed by the functor

{\operatorname{IndCoh}}(X_{1})\underset{{\operatorname{QCoh}}(S)}{\otimes}{\operatorname{IndCoh}}(X_{2})\to{\operatorname{IndCoh}}(X_{1}\underset{S}{\times}X_{2}).

Note that even if
S
,
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are smooth, the above functor is
not
an equivalence
as long as
X_{1}\underset{S}{\times}X_{2}
is not smooth.

Remark 0.3.6
.

Stefanich’s construction is an improvement and generalization of a construction that produces
the same 2-category
2\operatorname{-IndCoh}(S)
that had been proposed earlier by D. Arinkin.

This construction plays a key role in formulating the 2-categorical local geometric Langlands,
where we take
S
to be the stack of local Langlands parameters
\operatorname{LS}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\check{G}}
. The need for an enlargement

2\operatorname{-QCoh}(\operatorname{LS}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\check{G}})\rightsquigarrow 2\operatorname{-IndCoh}(\operatorname{LS}^{\operatorname{loc}}_{\check{G}})

is due to the phenomenon of
non-temperedness
, and is a local counterpart of the enlargement

{\operatorname{QCoh}}(\operatorname{LS}^{\operatorname{glob}}_{\check{G}})\rightsquigarrow{\operatorname{IndCoh}}(\operatorname{LS}^{\operatorname{glob}}_{\check{G}})

in global geometric Langlands.

0.4.
What else is done in this paper?

0.4.1.

As was mentioned in Sect.
0.2.2
, the formalism of
\operatorname{AGCat}
provides a nice answer for

\operatorname{Tr}(\underline{[{\mathcal{F}}]},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X)),\quad{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X\times X)

when
X
is a scheme.

However, for practical applications we need a similar formula when
X
is allowed to be a more general
algebro-geometric object, i.e., a
prestack
(assumed locally of finite type).
In order to do this, we need to address the following questions:

(1)

What is
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})\in\operatorname{AGCat}
for a prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
?

(2)

Under what conditions is it true that

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1})\otimes\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{2})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2})?

(3)

What guarantees that the above object
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})\in\operatorname{AGCat}
is dualizable
(so that the trace operation is defined)?

(4)

What guarantees that
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})
is self-dual?

(5)

If
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})
is self-dual and
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})\otimes\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{Y}})
,
is it true that

\operatorname{Tr}(\underline{[{\mathcal{F}}]},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}))\simeq\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}({\mathcal{Y}},\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}^{!}({\mathcal{F}})),,\quad{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{Y}})?

Answering these questions is what occupies the bulk of this paper.

0.4.2.

First, we define
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})
by

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})(X):=\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}\times X),

where the category of sheaves on a prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
is defined by

\underset{X\in(\operatorname{Sch}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}})^{\operatorname{op}}}{\operatorname{lim}}\,\operatorname{Shv}(X),

where the limit is formed using the !-pullback functors.

With this definition, there is a naturally defined map

(0.6)

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1})\otimes\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{2})\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}),

but it is not an equivalence in general.

0.4.3.

That said, one can associate to a prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
a different category, namely

\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime})_{\operatorname{co}}:=\underset{X\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{Shv}(X),

where the colimit is formed using the *-pushforward functors.

We define the object
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}\in\operatorname{AGCat}
by

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}(X)=\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}\times X)_{\operatorname{co}}.

If
{\mathcal{Y}}=X
is a scheme, then more or less by definition, we have

\operatorname{Shv}(X)_{\operatorname{co}}=\operatorname{Shv}(X)\text{ and }\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X)_{\operatorname{co}}=\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X),

but for a general prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
, the two are different. However, if
{\mathcal{Y}}

has a schematic diagonal, there are natural maps

\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}:\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}\to\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})\text{ and }\underline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}:\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}).

We call a prestack
tame
(resp., universally tame) if
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}
(resp.,
\underline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{Y}}
) is an equivalence.
We shall say that
{\mathcal{Y}}
is
AG tame
if both
{\mathcal{Y}}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}
are universally tame.

0.4.4.

Tame (resp., universally tame, AG tame) prestacks are quite abundant: any ind-scheme
(or, more generally, any pseudo-scheme) is AG tame.

And any quasi-compact algebraic stack with an affine diagonal, which is locally a quotient
of a scheme by a group, is AG tame. (In fact, conjecturally, the latter condition is not even
necessary).

0.4.5.

It turns out that AG tame prestacks are adapted for positive answers to the questions
posed in Sect.
0.4.1
:

(1)

If
{\mathcal{Y}}
is AG tame, then for any
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
, the map

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})\otimes\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime})\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime})

is an equivalence.

(2)

If
{\mathcal{Y}}
is AG tame, then the object
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})\in\operatorname{AGCat}
is dualizable and self-dual;

(3)

If
{\mathcal{Y}}
is AG tame, then for
{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{Y}})
,

(0.7)

\operatorname{Tr}(\underline{[{\mathcal{F}}]},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}))\simeq\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}_{\blacktriangle}({\mathcal{Y}},\Delta_{\mathcal{Y}}^{!}({\mathcal{F}})),

where
\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}_{\blacktriangle}({\mathcal{Y}},-)
is the functor of
renormalized
cochains,
see Sect.
5.2.2
.

0.5.
Structure of the paper

This paper is structured as follows:

0.5.1.

In Sect.
1
we explain the general construction

({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}},F)\mapsto\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}).

0.5.2.

In Sect.
2
we use the construction in Sect.
1
to produce
\operatorname{AGCat}
.

0.5.3.

In Sect.
3
we explain how to evaluate
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-)
on prestacks, and develop
the theory outlined in Secs.
0.4.2
-
0.4.5
.

0.5.4.

In Sect.
4
we explain how the notion of monoidal dual in
\operatorname{AGCat}
interacts
with Verdier duality on schemes and algebraic stacks.

0.5.5.

Finally, in Sect.
5
we perform the trace calculation (
0.7
). We then specialize
to the case of case of the Frobenius map (for algebraic stacks over
\overline{{{\symAMSb F}}}_{q}
) and show that

\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{*},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}))\simeq\mathsf{Funct}({\mathcal{Y}}({{\symAMSb F}}_{q}),\overline{{{\symAMSb Q}}}_{\ell}).

We also explain how the latter identification relates to Grothendieck’s sheaves-functionss correspondence.

0.5.6.

In Sect.
A
we review the theory of enriched categories (over a given monoidal category

{\mathcal{A}}
) and relate them to
{\mathcal{A}}
-module categories.

The key notions here are the
enriched presheaves
construction

({\mathcal{C}}\in\operatorname{Cat}^{\mathcal{A}})\mapsto(\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{C}})\in{\mathcal{A}}{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}).

and the enriched Yoneda functor

\operatorname{Yon}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{C}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{C}}).

0.5.7.

In Sect.
B
we review
weighted limits and colimits
,
which are generalizations of usual limits and colimits for
{\mathcal{A}}
-module
categories.

The weights in question are given by objects of
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{C}})
and
\mathbf{P}^{{\mathcal{A}}^{\operatorname{rev}}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\operatorname{op}})
,
respectively.

0.5.8.

Finally, in Sect.
C
we review the notion of adjunction in an
(\infty,2)
-category.

0.6.
Conventions and notations

0.6.1.

We will be working in the context of higher category theory. When we say “category”
we always mean an
\infty
-category.

We let
{\operatorname{Spc}}
denote the category of
spaces
, a.k.a.
groupoids
.

Given a category
{\mathbf{C}}
, and
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}
, we let

\operatorname{Maps}_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})\in{\operatorname{Spc}}

denote the corresponding space of objects.

For a category
{\mathbf{C}}
, we let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\operatorname{grpd}}
denote the space of its objects
(i.e., the groupoid obtained from
{\mathbf{C}}
by discarding non-invertible morphisms).

0.6.2.

In this paper we will also use
(\infty,2)
-categories. We will adopt the
definition developed in
[
GR1
, Chapter 10]
.

0.6.3.

Throughout this paper we will be working with the category of (classical, separated) schemes of finite type
over a ground field
k
, assumed algebraically closed. We denote this category by
\operatorname{Sch}
.
We will not need derived algebraic geometry over
k
for this paper.

By a prestack we shall mean a (classical) prestack locally of finite type over
k

(see
[
GR1
, Chapter 2, Sect. 1.3.6]
for what this means). We denote the corresponding category
by
\operatorname{PreStk}
.

0.6.4.

We will do
higher algebra
in vector spaces over the field of coefficients, which
in this paper we take to be
\overline{{{\symAMSb Q}}}_{\ell}
.

We let
\operatorname{DGCat}
denote the
(\infty,2)
-category of
\overline{{{\symAMSb Q}}}_{\ell}
-linear DG categories,
defined as in
[
GR1
, Chapter 1, Sect. 10]
.

We view
\operatorname{DGCat}
as equipped with the symmetric monoidal structure, given by the
Lurie tensor product. The unit object for this structure is
\operatorname{Vect}
,
the category of (chain complexes)
\overline{{{\symAMSb Q}}}_{\ell}
-vector spaces.

For a DG category
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}
, we let

{\mathcal{H}}\operatorname{om}_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})\in\operatorname{Vect}

denote the corresponding object. We have

\operatorname{Maps}_{{\mathbf{C}}}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})=\tau^{\leq 0}({\mathcal{H}}\operatorname{om}_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})),

where we view the right-hand side as a connective spectrum.

0.7.
Acknowledgements

This paper (obviously) owes its existence to V. Drinfeld.

We would also like to thank D. Kazhdan, S. Raskin, G. Stefanich for valuable discussions.

The research of N.R. is supported by an NSERC Discovery Grant (RGPIN-2025-0696).

The research of Y.V. was partially supported by the ISF grants
2091/21 and 2889/25.

1.
The enhancement construction

In this section we introduce a general categorical procedure, in the framework of which
we will define
\operatorname{AGCat}
. As was mentioned in the introduction (see Sect.
0.3.5
),
this procedure appears to have a broader use.

1.1.
Setup and construction

1.1.1.

Let
{\mathbf{O}}
be a closed
3
3

3

Recall that a (symmetric)
monoidal category is
closed
if it has internal homs; i.e. for every
x\in{\mathbf{O}}
, the functor
(-)\otimes x:{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathbf{O}}
admits a right adjoint.
symmetric monoidal category,
{\mathcal{V}}
a presentable symmetric monoidal category

4
4

4

I.e., an
commutative algebra object in
\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{pres}}
, see Sect.
A.2.7
.
and

F:{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathcal{V}}

a right-lax symmetric monoidal functor.

We will construct a symmetric monoidal
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
along with a symmetric monoidal functor

\underline{F}:{\mathbf{O}}\to\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})

together with an identification of right-lax symmetric monoidal functors

(1.1)

F\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}),{\mathcal{V}}}(\mathbb{1}_{\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})},-)\circ\underline{F},

where the notation
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}),{\mathcal{V}}}
stands for the
relative internal

Hom in
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
with respect to
{\mathcal{V}}
.

1.1.2.

As was explained in the introduction, the construction of the category
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
uses the theory of enriched categories.
In Sect.
A
, we provide a digest of the relevant aspects of the theory of enriched categories (in the
\infty
-categorical setting).

For a monoidal category
{\mathcal{A}}
, we will denote by
\operatorname{Cat}^{{\mathcal{A}}}
the category of
{\mathcal{A}}
-enriched categories. When
{\mathcal{A}}
is
symmetric
monoidal,

\operatorname{Cat}^{{\mathcal{A}}}
is naturally a symmetric monoidal category, see Sect.
A.10.1
.

We will also be considering module categories over a given presentable monoidal category
{\mathcal{A}}
(see Sect.
A.3.4
); we denote
the 2-category of such by
{\mathcal{A}}{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
. For
{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{A}}{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
and
m_{1},m_{2}\in{\mathcal{M}}
, we denote by

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{A}}}(m_{2},m_{2})\in{\mathcal{A}}

the
internal-relative-to

{\mathcal{A}}
hom, i.e.,

\operatorname{Maps}_{\mathcal{A}}(a,\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{A}}}(m_{2},m_{2})):=\operatorname{Maps}_{\mathcal{M}}(a\otimes m_{1},m_{2}).

The assignment

(m_{1},m_{2})\mapsto\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{A}}}(m_{2},m_{2})

makes
{\mathcal{M}}
into an object of
\operatorname{Cat}^{\mathcal{A}}
, whose underlying category is the original
{\mathcal{M}}
, i.e.,

\operatorname{Hom}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{M}}(m_{1},m_{2}):=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{A}}}(m_{2},m_{2}).

1.1.3.

By
[
GeHa
, Sect. 7]
(see also
[
He
]
), the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
{\mathbf{O}}
gives rise to a symmetric monoidal
{\mathbf{O}}
-enriched category

\operatorname{Self-Enr}({\mathbf{O}})\in\operatorname{ComAlg}(\operatorname{Cat}^{{\mathbf{O}}}).

The space of objects of
\operatorname{Self-Enr}({\mathbf{O}})
is the same as that of
{\mathbf{O}}
and for
{\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\in{\mathbf{O}}

\operatorname{Hom}^{\mathbf{O}}_{\operatorname{Self-Enr}({\mathbf{O}})}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})\in{\mathbf{O}}

is given by the internal hom in
{\mathbf{O}}
.

We have a symmetric monoidal equivalence

{\mathbf{oblv}}_{\operatorname{Enr}}(\operatorname{Self-Enr}({\mathbf{O}}))\simeq{\mathbf{O}},

where
{\mathbf{oblv}}_{\operatorname{Enr}}
is the functor that forgets enrichment (see Sect.
A.1.11
).

1.1.4.

We define

\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})\in\operatorname{Cat}^{{\mathcal{V}}}

to be the
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category obtained from
\operatorname{Self-Enr}({\mathbf{O}})
and changing enrichment by
F
, i.e.,

\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}):={\mathbf{ind}}_{\mathbf{O}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\operatorname{Self-Enr}({\mathbf{O}})).

Since
F
is right-lax
symmetric
monoidal,
\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
is naturally a symmetric monoidal
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category
(see Sect.
A.10.2
).

The category
\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
has the objects
flagged by
5
5

5

See Sect.
A.1
for what this means.

{\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}}
and morphism objects are given by

\operatorname{Hom}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})=F(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})),

with composition given by the right-lax monoidal structure of
F
. Moreover, tensor product in
\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
agrees with that in
{\mathbf{O}}

at the level of objects (see Sect.
A.10.2
).

We have a tautologically defined symmetric monoidal functor

(1.2)

{\mathbf{O}}\simeq{\mathbf{oblv}}_{{\mathbf{O}}\operatorname{-Enr}}(\operatorname{Self-Enr}({\mathbf{O}}))\to{\mathbf{oblv}}_{{\mathcal{V}}\operatorname{-Enr}}(\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})).

1.1.5.

For any
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category
{\mathcal{C}}
, there is a corresponding
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category

(1.3)

\mathbf{P}^{{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{C}}):=\operatorname{Funct}^{{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\operatorname{op}},{\mathcal{V}})

of
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors from the opposite category of
{\mathcal{C}}
to
{\mathcal{V}}
. As in the unenriched category theory, this
category has the universal property that it is the free cocompletion of
{\mathcal{C}}
as a
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category
(see Proposition
A.5.6
for the precise assertion).

1.1.6.

We define
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
as the enriched presheaf category:

\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}):=\mathbf{P}^{{\mathcal{V}}}(\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})).

The symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
induces one on
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
, see Sect.
A.10.5
.

Composing (
1.2
) with the enriched Yoneda embedding (see Sect.
A.6
),
we obtain the desired symmetric monoidal functor

\underline{F}:{\mathbf{O}}\to\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}).

Sometimes, when no confusion is likely to occur, we will write

\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}:=\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}).

1.1.7.

By the enriched Yoneda lemma (Corollary
A.6.5
), for
{\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\in{\mathbf{O}}
,

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}},{\mathcal{V}}}(\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}}_{1}),\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}}_{2}))\simeq\operatorname{Hom}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2}):=F(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{O}}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})).

In particular,

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}},{\mathcal{V}}}(\mathbb{1}_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}},\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}}))\simeq F({\mathbf{o}}),

as desired, see (
1.1
).

1.1.8.

Using (
1.3
), we will regard an object
\underline{v}\in\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}

as an assignment

{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}\mapsto\underline{v}({\mathbf{o}})\in{\mathcal{V}},

together with compatible system of maps

F(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2}))\otimes\underline{v}({\mathbf{o}}_{2})\to\underline{v}({\mathbf{o}}_{1}).

For example, for
\underline{v}=\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}}^{\prime})
, we have

(1.4)

\underline{v}({\mathbf{o}})=\operatorname{Hom}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})}({\mathbf{o}},{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime})=F(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{O}}({\mathbf{o}},{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime})).

By the enriched Yoneda lemma (Lemma
A.6.3
), for
\underline{v}\in\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}
and
{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}
,

\underline{v}({\mathbf{o}})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}},{\mathcal{V}}}(\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}}),\underline{v}).

1.2.
Basic properties of the enhancement construction

1.2.1.

Let
{\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}}
denote the space of objects of
{\mathbf{O}}
. By Sect.
A.9.1
, we have a monadic adjunction in
{\mathcal{V}}\mathbf{\mbox{-}mod}
.

(1.5)

\textstyle{\operatorname{Funct}({\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}},{\mathcal{V}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})=\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}.}

In terms of Sect.
1.1.8
, the right adjoint in
Equation

1.5
sends
\underline{v}\in\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}

to its restriction along
{\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{O}}
.

1.2.2.

In particular, we obtain that the right adjoint in
Equation

1.5
is conservative, and it follows from
Appendix

B
that
it preserves limits and colimits and tensors and cotensors
6
6

6

See Sect.
B.1.6
for what this means.

with objects of
{\mathcal{V}}
.

By Proposition
B.2.4
, this implies that the right adjoint in
Equation

1.5
preserves all
weighted limits and colimits.

In other words, when we view objects of
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
as in Sect.
1.1.8
, weighted limits and colimits
are computed value-wise.

1.2.3.

Consider the functor

{\mathcal{V}}\times{\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}}\overset{\operatorname{Id}\otimes\operatorname{Yon}_{\mathbf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{V}}\otimes\operatorname{Funct}({\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}},{\operatorname{Spc}})\to\operatorname{Funct}({\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}},{\mathcal{V}}).

The images of the objects
v\times{\mathbf{o}}
for
{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}}
,
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
generate the category
\operatorname{Funct}({\mathbf{O}}^{\operatorname{grpd}},{\mathcal{V}})
.
It follows that the corresponding objects

v\otimes\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}})\in\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}

generate
\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}
under colimits (see Corollary
A.9.4
and Sect.
B.4.1
for more precise assertions to this effect).

1.2.4.

We observe:

Proposition 1.2.5
.

Suppose that every object of
{\mathbf{O}}
is dualizable. Then
the symmetric monoidal category
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
is closed; i.e. admits internal homs.

Proof.

Since every object in
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
is a weighted colimit of
\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}})
for
{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}
and
\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
admits all weighted
limits
,
by Proposition
B.3.3
, it suffices to show that for an object
\underline{v}\in\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
, there exists an internal hom

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})}(\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}}),\underline{v})\in\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}).

Since the functor
\underline{F}:{\mathbf{O}}\to\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
is symmetric monoidal and
{\mathbf{o}}
is dualizable, the object
\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}})
is dualizable. Hence,
the above internal Hom exists and equals

\underline{F}({\mathbf{o}}^{\vee})\otimes\underline{v}.

∎

1.2.6.

By the construction of
\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}:=\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})
, we have a right-lax symmetric monoidal adjunction of
{\mathcal{V}}
-module categories

(1.6)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{i}}:{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}:{\mathbf{e}}}

given by
{\mathbf{i}}(v)=v\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}}
and

{\mathbf{e}}(\underline{v})=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}},{\mathcal{V}}}(\mathbb{1}_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}},\underline{v})\simeq\underline{v}(\mathbb{1}_{{\mathbf{O}}}),

see Sect.
1.1.8
for the notation.

Note that by definition, for
{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}

(1.7)

{\mathbf{i}}(v)({\mathbf{o}})=v\otimes F(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}({\mathbf{o}},\mathbb{1}_{{\mathbf{O}}})).

1.2.7.

In practice, the functor
{\mathbf{i}}
is often fully faithful.

Proposition 1.2.8
.

Suppose that the functor
F
is strictly unital, i.e. the natural map

\mathbb{1}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\to F(\mathbb{1}_{{\mathbf{O}}})

is an isomorphism. Then the functor
{\mathbf{i}}
is fully faithful.

Proof.

By assumption, the natural map

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\mathbb{1}_{{\mathcal{V}}},\mathbb{1}_{{\mathcal{V}}})\to\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\underline{{\mathcal{V}}},{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathbf{i}}(\mathbb{1}_{{\mathcal{V}}}),{\mathbf{i}}(\mathbb{1}_{{\mathcal{V}}}))

is an isomorphism. Hence, by

B.4.8
, the functor

{\mathbf{i}}:{\mathcal{V}}\simeq\mathbf{P}^{{\mathcal{V}}}(*)\to\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})=\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}

is fully faithful.
∎

1.2.9.

We now show that the general set-up of
F:{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
can be reduced to one of Proposition
1.2.8
:

Note that the functor
F
induces a right-lax symmetric monoidal functor

F^{\operatorname{enh}}:{\mathbf{O}}\to F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}}),

so that the original
F
is the composition

{\mathbf{O}}\overset{F^{\operatorname{enh}}}{\longrightarrow}F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}})\overset{{\mathbf{oblv}}_{F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{V}}.

Note that the functor
F^{\operatorname{enh}}
is strictly unital.

Proposition 1.2.10
.

There is a natural equivalence of
{\mathcal{V}}
-module categories

\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}}))\simeq\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}).

Proof.

We have a right-lax symmetric monoidal adjunction

(1.8)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}})}

By functoriality of the change of enrichment, the counit of the adjunction
Equation

1.8

induces a functor

(1.9)

{\mathbf{ind}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}})}(\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}}))\to\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}})).

Since the left adjoint in Sect.
1.8
is
strictly
symmetric monoidal, by Sect.
A.5.7
,
passing to enriched presheaves, the functor (
1.9
) gives rise to a functor of
(F(\mathbb{1})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}}))
-module categories

F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}})\underset{{\mathcal{V}}}{\otimes}\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})\to\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}})).

By adjunction, this gives rise to a functor of
{\mathcal{V}}
-module categories

(1.10)

\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})\to\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}},F(\mathbb{1}_{\mathbf{O}})\operatorname{-mod}({\mathcal{V}})).

By construction, the functor
Equation

1.10
maps representable objects to representable objects and is
fully faithful (in the enriched sense) on representable objects. Thus, by
B.4.9
it is an equivalence.
∎

1.3.
The universal property

1.3.1.

The isomorphism

F\simeq{\mathbf{e}}\circ\underline{F}

gives rise to a natural transformation

{\mathbf{i}}\circ F\overset{\alpha}{\to}\underline{F}

as right-lax symmetric monoidal functors
{\mathbf{O}}\to\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}
.

1.3.2.

We claim that the above data
(\underline{F},{\mathbf{i}},\alpha)
satisfy the following universal property:

Proposition 1.3.3
.

For a presentable symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
, precomposition with the triple
(F,{\mathbf{i}},\alpha)
defines an
equivalence between:

(i) The groupoid of colimit-preserving symmetric monoidal functors
\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
;

(ii) The groupoid of triples
(G,{\mathbf{j}},\beta)
, where:

•

G
is a symmetric monoidal functor
{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
;

•

{\mathbf{j}}
is a colimit-preserving symmetric monoidal functor
{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
;

•

\beta
is a natural transformation
{\mathbf{j}}\circ F\to G

as right-lax symmetric monoidal functors
{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
.

Proof.

We construct a map in the opposite direction.

Let us be given a colimit-preserving symmetric monoidal functor
{\mathbf{j}}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
. In particular,
we can view
{\mathbf{C}}
a commutative algebra object in
\operatorname{Cat}^{\mathcal{V}}
.

Then, by Proposition
A.5.6
, the datum of a colimit-preserving symmetric monoidal functor
\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is equivalent to that
of a symmetric monoidal
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched functor

\operatorname{enh}({\mathbf{O}},{\mathcal{V}})\to{\mathbf{C}}.

By the universal property in Sect.
A.1.5
, the latter is the same as a symmetric monoidal functor

\operatorname{Self-Enr}({\mathbf{O}})\to{\mathbf{C}}

compatible with
F
. In particular, this produces a symmetric monoidal functor
G:{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
.

The structure on
G
of compatibility with
F
amounts to maps

F(\operatorname{Hom}^{\mathbf{O}}_{\operatorname{Enr}({\mathbf{O}})}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2}))\to\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}},{\mathcal{V}}}(G({\mathbf{o}}_{1}),G({\mathbf{o}}_{2})),

compatible with the symmetric monoidal structure.

The latter structure is equivalent to a natural transformation

F\to j^{R}\circ G

as right-lax symmetric monoidal functors
{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
, or which is the same, as a natural transformation

j\circ F\to G

as right-lax symmetric monoidal functors
{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
.

∎

1.3.4.

Here is an application of Proposition
1.3.3
:

Proposition 1.3.5
.

Suppose that
F
is (strictly) symmetric monoidal and every object in
{\mathbf{O}}
is dualizable. Then the adjunction
Equation

1.6
is an equivalence.

Proof.

It suffices to show that (under the assumptions on
{\mathbf{O}}
and
F
), in the situation of
Proposition
1.3.3
, the natural transformation
\beta
is automatically an isomorphism.

This follows from the fact that a natural transformation between two (strict) symmetric monoidal functors

G_{1}\to G_{2},\quad G_{i}:{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathbf{C}}

is an isomorphism, provided that every object of
{\mathbf{O}}
is dualizable.

∎

1.4.
Change of source

1.4.1.

Let us now be given two pairs
({\mathbf{O}}_{1},F_{1}:{\mathbf{O}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}})
and
({\mathbf{O}}_{2},F_{2}:{\mathbf{O}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{V}})
.
Denote

\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{1}:=\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}}_{1},{\mathcal{V}})\text{ and }\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{2}:=\operatorname{Enh}({\mathbf{O}}_{2},{\mathcal{V}}).

Let us be given a symmetric monoidal functor
\Phi:{\mathbf{O}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{O}}_{2}
, equipped with a symmetric monoidal
natural transformation

(1.11)

F_{1}\to F_{2}\circ\Phi,

as right-lax symmetric monoidal functors
{\mathbf{O}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{V}}
.

1.4.2.

We claim that in this case there a naturally defined symmetric monoidal functor

\underline{\Phi}:\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{1}\to\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{2}

equipped with the identifications

\underline{F_{2}}\circ\Phi\simeq\underline{\Phi}\circ\underline{F_{1}}\text{ and }{\mathbf{i}}_{2}\simeq\underline{\Phi}\circ{\mathbf{i}}_{1}

that make the following diagram commute:

\begin{CD}\underline{\Phi}\circ{\mathbf{i}}_{1}\circ F_{1}&@>{\alpha_{1}}>{}>&\underline{\Phi}\circ\underline{F_{1}}\\
@V{\sim}V{}V&&@V{\sim}V{}V\\
{\mathbf{i}}_{2}\circ F_{1}@>{}>{}>{\mathbf{i}}_{2}\circ F_{2}\circ\Phi @>{\alpha_{2}}>{}>\underline{F_{2}}\circ\Phi.\end{CD}

1.4.3.

Indeed, this follows from Proposition
1.3.3
, where we take

{\mathbf{C}}:=\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{2},\,\,G=\underline{F_{2}}\circ\Phi,\,\,{\mathbf{j}}={\mathbf{i}}_{2}

and
\beta
be the composite bottom horizontal arrow in the above diagram.

1.4.4.

Note that from Proposition
B.4.7
, we obtain:

Corollary 1.4.5
.

Suppose that all objects in
{\mathbf{O}}_{1}
are dualizable and (
1.11
) is an isomorphism. Then the functor
\underline{\Phi}

is fully faithful.

2.
Construction of
\operatorname{AGCat}

In this section we construct the
(\infty,2)
-category
\operatorname{AGCat}
and study its basic properties.

2.1.
The category of correspondences

2.1.1.

Given a category
{\mathbf{C}}
with finite limits (i.e., fiber products and a final object), we let
\operatorname{Corr}({\mathbf{C}})
be the category of correspondences as
defined in
[
GR1
, Chapter 7, Sect. 1]
.

Explicitly, objects of
\operatorname{Corr}({\mathbf{C}})
are the same as objects of
{\mathbf{C}}
. For a pair of objects
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2}
,
the groupoid of morphisms
\operatorname{Maps}_{\operatorname{Corr}({\mathbf{C}})}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})
consists of diagrams

(2.1)

\begin{CD}{\mathbf{c}}_{1,2}@>{}>{}>{\mathbf{c}}_{2}\\
@V{}V{}V\\
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},\end{CD}

with the composition given by

({\mathbf{c}}_{1,2},{\mathbf{c}}_{2,3})\mapsto{\mathbf{c}}_{1,2}\underset{{\mathbf{c}}_{2}}{\times}{\mathbf{c}}_{2,3}.

We will refer to the horizontal morphism in (
2.1
) as the
forward
arrow, and
the vertical one as the
backward
arrow.

2.1.2.

The category
\operatorname{Corr}({\mathbf{C}})
has a natural symmetric monoidal structure, induced by the Cartesian
product in
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Note that every object in
\operatorname{Corr}({\mathbf{C}})
is dualizable and canonically self-dual. Namely,
the unit and counit of the self-duality are given by

\begin{CD}{\mathbf{c}}@>{\Delta_{\mathbf{C}}}>{}>{\mathbf{c}}\times{\mathbf{c}}\\
@V{}V{}V\\
\{*\}\end{CD}

and

\begin{CD}{\mathbf{c}}@>{}>{}>\{*\}\\
@V{\Delta_{\mathbf{C}}}V{}V\\
{\mathbf{c}}\times{\mathbf{c}},\end{CD}

respectively, where
\{*\}
is the final object in
{\mathbf{C}}
.

In particular, we obtain that
\operatorname{Corr}({\mathbf{C}})
is closed and for
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}
, the internal hom
object
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\operatorname{Corr}({\mathbf{C}})}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})
is
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}\times{\mathbf{c}}_{2}
.

2.1.3.

Let
\operatorname{class}_{1}
and
\operatorname{class}_{2}
be two classes of morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}
, each stable
under composition, and under base change with respect to each other, i.e., for a Cartesian square

\begin{CD}{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}_{1}@>{f^{\prime}}>{}>{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}_{2}\\
@V{g_{1}}V{}V@V{}V{g_{2}}V\\
{\mathbf{c}}_{1}@>{f}>{}>{\mathbf{c}}_{2}\end{CD},

if
f\in\operatorname{class}_{1}
and
g_{2}\in\operatorname{class}_{2}
, then
f^{\prime}\in\operatorname{class}_{1}
and
g_{1}\in\operatorname{class}_{2}
.

Then we define a 1-full subcategory

\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{class}_{1},\operatorname{class}_{2}}({\mathbf{C}})\subset\operatorname{Corr}({\mathbf{C}}),

where we restrict morphisms to those diagrams (
2.1
), where
the forward morphism belongs to
\operatorname{class}_{1}
and the backward
morphism belongs to
\operatorname{class}_{2}
.

2.1.4.

For
\operatorname{class}_{1}=\operatorname{all}
and
\operatorname{class}_{2}=\operatorname{isom}
, we obtain
an equivalence

\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{all},\operatorname{isom}}({\mathbf{C}})={\mathbf{C}}

and for
\operatorname{class}_{1}=\operatorname{isom}
and
\operatorname{class}_{2}=\operatorname{class}
, we obtain
an equivalence

\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{isom},\operatorname{all}}({\mathbf{C}})={\mathbf{C}}^{\operatorname{op}}.

2.2.
The functor
\operatorname{Shv}(-)

2.2.1.

Let
B
be a base scheme, and consider the category
\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})
.

The sheaf-theoretic input to the construction of the category
\operatorname{AGCat}
is provided by a functor

(2.2)

\operatorname{Shv}(-):\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\to\operatorname{DGCat},

which is a
constructible sheaf theory
in the sense of
[
AGKRRV1
, Sect. 1.1.4]
.

2.2.2.

This functor assigns to a scheme
X
over
B
the corresponding category
\operatorname{Shv}(X)
and to a morphism

f:X_{1}\to X_{2}
the functors

f_{*}:\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1})\to\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})\text{ and }f^{!}:\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})\to\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1}).

Thus, the restriction
\operatorname{Shv}(-)|_{\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{isom,all}}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})}

is the functor

(2.3)

(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{DGCat},\quad X\mapsto\operatorname{Shv}(X),\quad(X_{1}\overset{f}{\to}X_{2})\mapsto f^{!},

and the restriction
\operatorname{Shv}(-)|_{\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{all,isom}}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})}

is the functor

(2.4)

\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}\to\operatorname{DGCat},\quad X\mapsto\operatorname{Shv}(X),\quad(X_{1}\overset{f}{\to}X_{2})\mapsto f_{*}.

2.2.3.

The datum of extension of these functors to a functor (
2.2
) amounts to a homotopy-compatible
system of base-change isomorphisms: for

\begin{CD}X_{1}\underset{X_{2}}{\times}X^{\prime}_{2}@>{f^{\prime}}>{}>X^{\prime}_{2}\\
@V{g_{1}}V{}V@V{}V{g_{2}}V\\
X_{1}@>{f}>{}>X_{2}\end{CD},

an isomorphism

g_{2}^{!}\circ f_{*}\simeq f^{\prime}_{*}\circ g_{1}^{!},

as functors
\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1})\to\operatorname{Shv}(X^{\prime}_{2})
.

The gist of the work
[
GR1
]
can be summarized as follows: the datum of just (
2.4
)
(or (
2.3
)) uniquely recovers (
2.2
).

2.2.4.

For this paper, we let (
2.2
) be the theory of
\ell
-adic sheaves, defined as in
[
GKRV
, Sect. A.1.1(d’)]
,
followed by tensoring up
(-)\underset{{{\symAMSb Q}}_{\ell}}{\otimes}\overline{{{\symAMSb Q}}}_{\ell}
.

2.3.
Construction of
\operatorname{AGCat}

2.3.1.

Morally, we would like to apply the set-up of Sect.
1.1
to

{\mathbf{O}}:=\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}),\,\,{\mathcal{V}}=\operatorname{DGCat},\,\,F:=\operatorname{Shv}(-).

Unfortunately, we cannot quite do that, because in Sect.
1.1
we assumed that

{\mathcal{V}}
is presentable, while
\operatorname{DGCat}
is not.

To circumvent this, we will employ the recipe of Sect.
A.2.8
.

2.3.2.

In the setting of Sect.
1.1
, we take

{\mathbf{O}}:=\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}),\,\,{\mathcal{V}}=\operatorname{DGCat}_{\kappa},\,\,F:=\operatorname{Shv}(-),

where
\kappa
is as in Sect.
A.2.4
.

Set

(2.5)

\operatorname{AGCat}_{\kappa,/B}:=\operatorname{Enh}(\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}),\operatorname{DGCat}_{\kappa}).

(2.6)

\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}:=\underset{\kappa<\lambda_{0}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{AGCat}_{\kappa,/B},

where the colimit is taken in
\operatorname{Cat}_{\operatorname{large}}
,
cf. Sect.
A.2.8
.

Remark 2.3.3
.

From this point on, in the main body of the paper, we will ignore the difference between

\operatorname{DGCat}
and
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\kappa}
, and pretend that the construction in Sect.
1.1

was applied directly that
\operatorname{DGCat}
.

We leave it to the reader to verify that the constructions and statements that follow stay
within our specified set-theoretic framework.

Remark 2.3.4
.

When
B=\operatorname{pt}=\operatorname{Spec}(k)
, we will write simply
\operatorname{AGCat}
instead of
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
.

2.4.
Basic properties of
\operatorname{AGCat}

2.4.1.

By construction, the category
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
comes equipped with the following data:

•

A symmetric monoidal functor

(2.7)

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-):\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\to\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B};

•

An adjunction

(2.8)

{\mathbf{i}}:\operatorname{DGCat}\rightleftarrows\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}:{\mathbf{e}},

with
{\mathbf{i}}
(strictly) symmetric monoidal;

•

An identification
\operatorname{Shv}(-)\simeq{\mathbf{e}}\circ\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-)
as right-lax symmetric monoidal functors.

The above data give rise to a natural transformation

\alpha:{\mathbf{i}}\circ\operatorname{Shv}(-)\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-).

The triple
(\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-),{\mathbf{i}},\alpha)
has a universal property formulated in Sect.
1.3
. I.e.,
we can think of
(\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-))
as a universal way of turning
\operatorname{Shv}(-)
into being strictly
monoidal.

2.4.2.

As explained in
Section

1.1.8
, we will regard objects of
\operatorname{AGCat}
as functors

\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}:\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}^{\operatorname{grpd}}\to\operatorname{DGCat}

endowed with additional structure: namely, for every
X_{1},X_{2}\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}
, and every sheaf

{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1}\underset{B}{\times}X_{2}),

of a functor

(2.9)

\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}({\mathcal{F}}):\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{1})\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{2})

that is natural in all parameters.

2.4.3.

From this point of view, for an object
Z\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}
, the object

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(Z)\in\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
is given by the assignment

\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}\ni X\mapsto\operatorname{Shv}(Z\underset{B}{\times}X)

with the functors

\operatorname{Shv}(Z\underset{B}{\times}X_{1})\to\operatorname{Shv}(Z\underset{B}{\times}X_{2}),\quad{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1}\underset{B}{\times}X_{2})

given by convolution:

{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto[{\mathcal{F}}],\quad[{\mathcal{F}}]({\mathcal{F}}_{1}):=(p_{2})_{*}(p_{1}^{!}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\overset{!}{\otimes}p^{!}_{1,2}({\mathcal{F}})),

where
p_{1},p_{2},p_{1,2}
are the three projections from
Z\underset{B}{\times}X_{1}\underset{B}{\times}X_{2}
to

Z\underset{B}{\times}X_{1},\,\,Z\underset{B}{\times}X_{2},\,\,X_{1}\underset{B}{\times}X_{2},

respectively.

2.4.4.

The data of (
2.9
) can be rewritten as follows:

•

For every
X
, the category
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X)
is acted on by
\operatorname{Shv}(X)
, which is regarded as a (symmetric) monoidal
category with respect to the
\overset{!}{\otimes}
operation;

•

For every
f:X_{1}\to X_{2}
(over
B
), we have a pullback functor
f^{!}:\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{2})\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{1})
, which is
\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})
-linear
with respect to
f^{!}:\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})\to\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1})
.

•

For every
f:X_{1}\to X_{2}
, we have a pushforward functor
f_{*}:\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{1})\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{2})
, which is
\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})
-linear
with respect to
f^{!}:\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})\to\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1})
.

•

For a diagram

\begin{CD}X_{1}\underset{Y}{\times}X_{2}@>{f^{\prime}_{1}}>{}>X_{2}\\
@V{f^{\prime}_{2}}V{}V@V{}V{f_{2}}V\\
X_{1}@>{f_{1}}>{}>Y\end{CD}

an identification

(f^{\prime}_{1})_{*}\circ(f^{\prime}_{2})^{!}\simeq(f_{2})^{!}\circ(f_{1})_{*},\quad\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{1})\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(X_{2});

•

A homotopy-coherent system of compatibilities between the above data.

2.4.5.
Example

For
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}=\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(Z)
, the data from Sect.
2.4.4
is particularly evident.

2.4.6.

In terms of the description in Sect.
2.4.2
, the adjoint functors (
2.8
) look
as follows:

For
{\mathbf{C}}\in\operatorname{DGCat}
,

{\mathbf{i}}({\mathbf{C}})(X)={\mathbf{C}}\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(X)

and

{\mathbf{e}}(\underline{{\mathbf{C}}})=\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}(B).

The natural transformation

\alpha:{\mathbf{i}}\circ\operatorname{Shv}(-)\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-)

assigns to
Z\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}
the collection of functors

\operatorname{Shv}(Z)\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(X)\to\operatorname{Shv}(Z\underset{B}{\times}X).

2.4.7.

The category
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
has limits/colimits and tensors/cotensors by objects of
\operatorname{DGCat}
.
By Sect.
1.2.2
, all these operations are computed value-wise in terms of (
2.4.2
).

Additionally, thanks to Proposition
1.2.5
,
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
is closed; i.e. admits internal homs.

2.4.8.

By
1.2.10
, the construction of
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
is not very sensitive to the target of the functor

\operatorname{Shv}(-)
. In particular, we would get the same category if we replace

\operatorname{DGCat}\simeq\operatorname{Vect}\operatorname{-mod}(\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{pres}})

by
\operatorname{Cat}^{\operatorname{pres}}
or
\operatorname{Shv}(B)\operatorname{-mod}(\operatorname{DGCat})
.

In any case, the functor
{\mathbf{i}}
extends to a symmetric monoidal functor

\operatorname{Shv}(B){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}(\operatorname{DGCat})\to\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B},

and the latter is fully faithful (see Propositions
1.2.8
and
1.2.10
).

2.5.
Variants

We can consider several variants of the category
\operatorname{AGCat}
.

2.5.1.
D-module variant

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. To a scheme
X
over
k
, we can attach the category of D-modules
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)
.
These assemble to a (strictly) symmetric monoidal functor

\operatorname{D-mod}(-):\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{k})\to\operatorname{DGCat}_{k},

and we can perform the same construction as above.

However, by
1.3.5
, there is an equivalence

\operatorname{Enh}(\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{k}),\operatorname{DGCat}_{k})\simeq\operatorname{DGCat}_{k}.

This suggests the following guiding meta-principle for much of this work: theorems about categories about
D-modules should hold in the
\ell
-adic setting when categories of sheaves are interpreted as objects of
\operatorname{AGCat}
.

2.5.2.
Affine schemes are enough

Instead of considering the source of
F
to be all schemes over
B
, we can instead consider only affine schemes.
As we shall presently see, this produces an equivalent category.

Let
\operatorname{Shv}|_{\operatorname{aff}}(-)
denote the restriction of the functor
\operatorname{Shv}(-)
to the (1-full) subcategory

\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}^{\operatorname{aff}})\subset\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}),

and let

\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}^{\operatorname{aff}}:=\operatorname{Enh}(\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{/B}),\operatorname{DGCat}).

By Sect.
1.4.2
, we have a naturally defined functor

(2.10)

\operatorname{AGCat}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{/B}\to\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}.

Moreover, by Corollary
1.4.5
(and Sect.
2.1.2
), the functor (
2.10
)
is fully faithful. We claim:

Proposition 2.5.3
.

The functor
Equation

2.10
is an equivalence.

Proof.

By Corollary
B.4.9
, it suffices to show that for every
Z\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}
, the object

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(Z)\in\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}

is in the essential image of the functor
Equation

2.10
. By construction, this is the case if
X
is affine.

Now, every scheme
Z
is a sifted colimit (in the category of Zariski sheaves) of affine schemes

Z\simeq\underset{i\in I}{\operatorname{colim}}\ S_{i}.

The operation of *-pushforward give rise to a functor

(2.11)

\underset{i\in I}{\operatorname{colim}}\ \underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(S_{i})\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(Z)

in
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
. Since
Equation

2.10
commutes with colimits, it is enough to show that (
2.11
)
is an equivalence.

Thus, by Sect.
2.4.7
, we have to show that (
2.11
) evaluates to an equivalence on every
X\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}
. I.e.,
we have to show that the functor

(2.12)

\underset{i\in I}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{Shv}(S_{i}\underset{B}{\times}X)\to\operatorname{Shv}(Z\underset{B}{\times}X)

is an equivalence.

The transition functors in (
2.12
) preserve compactness. Hence, the left-hand side in (
2.12
)
is compactly generated and its dual identifies with

\underset{i\in I^{\operatorname{op}}}{\operatorname{lim}}\,(\operatorname{Shv}(S_{i}\times X))^{\vee}.

Using Verdier duality (see Sect.
4.3
), we identify the functor dual to (
2.12
) with

(2.13)

\operatorname{Shv}(Z\underset{B}{\times}X)\to\underset{i\in I^{\operatorname{op}}}{\operatorname{lim}}\,\operatorname{Shv}(S_{i}\underset{B}{\times}X),

where in the right-hand side, the transition functors are given by !-restriction.

Thus, it suffices to show that (
2.13
) is an equivalence. However,
this follows from Zariski descent for
\operatorname{Shv}(-)
.

∎

In light of
2.5.3
, in the sequel we will sometimes restrict to affine schemes as test objects for

\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
.

2.5.4.
The
left
version of
\operatorname{AGCat}

In the setting of
\ell
-adic sheaves, there is another version of the category
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
. Namely, we have another functor

\operatorname{Shv}^{\operatorname{left}}(-):\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\to\operatorname{DGCat}

which takes a scheme
X
to
\operatorname{Shv}(X)
and morphisms given by
*
-pullback and
!
-pushforward.

This functor can be formally expressed in terms of
\operatorname{Shv}
as follows. The value of
\operatorname{Shv}
on every morphism in
\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})
admits a left adjoint.
Let

\operatorname{Shv}^{L}(-):\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{DGCat}

denote the functor obtained from
\operatorname{Shv}
by passing to left adjoints. Now, the functor
\operatorname{Shv}^{\operatorname{left}}(-)
is given by the composite

\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\simeq\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})^{\operatorname{op}}\overset{\operatorname{Shv}^{L}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{DGCat}

where the anti-equivalence of
\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})
is given by reading correspondences backwards (equivalently, it corresponding to passing to duals).

We define

\operatorname{AGCat}^{\operatorname{left}}_{/B}:=\operatorname{Enh}(\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B}),\operatorname{DGCat}),

formed using the above functor
\operatorname{Shv}^{\operatorname{left}}(-)
.

As before, we have a symmetric monoidal functor

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{left}}(-):\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\to\operatorname{AGCat}^{\operatorname{left}}_{/B}.

The contents of the previous two sections carry over to the present setting without any change
7
7

7

The two versions are, however,
substantially different from the point of view of interaction of monoidal duality on
\operatorname{AGCat}
and Verdier duality on schemes discussed in Sect.
4
.
.

Remark 2.5.5
.

For most applications (such as those involving the sheaves-functions correspondence),
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
is the more useful category.

Yet, there are some instances (notably ones that have to do with cohomology of shtukas), where it is more convenient to
use
\operatorname{AGCat}^{\operatorname{left}}_{/B}
.

2.6.
Change of base

2.6.1.

Let
\phi:B^{\prime}\to B
be a map between base schemes. Base change defines a symmetric monoidal functor

\Phi:\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\to\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B^{\prime}}).

In addition, !-pullback defines a symmetric monoidal natural transformation:

(2.14)

\operatorname{Shv}(-)\to\operatorname{Shv}(\Phi(-)).

2.6.2.

Hence, applying Sect.
1.4.2
, we obtain a symmetric monoidal functor

\underline{\Phi}:\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}\to\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B^{\prime}}

equipped with identifications

{\mathbf{i}}^{\prime}\simeq\underline{\Phi}\circ{\mathbf{i}}\text{ and }\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(\Phi(-))\simeq\underline{\Phi}\circ\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-).

In addition, from Corollary
1.4.5
we obtain:

Corollary 2.6.3
.

Suppose that (
2.14
) is an isomorphism. Then the functor

\underline{\Phi}
is fully faithful.

2.6.4.

Let now
k^{\prime}\supset k
be an algebraically closed field extension; set
B^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}(k^{\prime})\underset{\operatorname{Spec}(k)}{\times}B
. Base change gives rise to a
symmetric functor

\Phi:\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\to\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B^{\prime}})

and a symmetric monoidal natural transformation:

(2.15)

\operatorname{Shv}(-)\to\operatorname{Shv}(\Phi(-)).

Applying Sect.
1.4.2
again, we obtain a symmetric monoidal functor

\underline{\Phi}:\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}\to\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B^{\prime}}

equipped with identifications

{\mathbf{i}}^{\prime}\simeq\underline{\Phi}\circ{\mathbf{i}}\text{ and }\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(\Phi(-))\simeq\underline{\Phi}\circ\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-).

If (
2.15
) is an isomorphism, then
\underline{\Phi}
is fully faithful.

2.7.
Adjunctions in
\operatorname{AGCat}

2.7.1.

The category
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
is tensored over
\operatorname{DGCat}
, and in particular
\operatorname{Cat}
; hence, it naturally has the structure of a 2-category (see
Section

C.2.1
).

We have the following characterization of adjunctions in
\operatorname{AGCat}
.

Proposition 2.7.2
.

Let
F:\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}
be a 1-morphism in
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
. Then
F
admits a left (resp. right) adjoint if and only if
for every scheme
X
, the corresponding functor

\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}(X)\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}(X)

admits a left (resp. right) adjoint, and for every scheme
X^{\prime}
and a
constructible sheaf

{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X\underset{B}{\times}X^{\prime})^{c}
, the square

\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}(X^{\prime})}

satisfies the left (resp. right) Beck-Chevalley condition (see
Section

C.1.3
).

Proof.

We prove the assertion for the left adjoint (the proof is identical for the right adjoint).

Suppose the conditions are satisfied.
By Proposition
C.2.2
, it remains to show that for every scheme
X^{\prime}
, the diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname{Shv}(X\times X^{\prime})\otimes\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Shv}(X\times X^{\prime})\otimes\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}(X^{\prime})}

satisfies the left Beck-Chevalley condition.

The category
\operatorname{Shv}(X\times X^{\prime})\otimes\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}(X)
is generated under colimits by objects of the form

{\mathcal{F}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{c}}_{2}
for
{\mathcal{F}}\in\operatorname{Shv}(X\times X^{\prime})^{c}

and
{\mathbf{c}}_{2}\in\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}(X)
. Thus, since all functors preserve colimits,
it suffices to check the Beck-Chevalley condition on these objects. This is exactly the condition in the proposition.

∎

2.7.3.

C.1.5
gives a general characterization of functors from the 2-category
\operatorname{Adj}
. Specializing to the target
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
, we have

Proposition 2.7.4
.

The restriction functor

\operatorname{Funct}(\operatorname{Adj},\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B})\to\operatorname{Funct}([1],\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B})

is 1-full and the essential image is characterized as follows:

(a)
An object
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}\in\operatorname{Funct}([1],\operatorname{AGCat})
is in the essential image if and only if
it admits a right (resp. left) adjoint in
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
;

(b)
A morphism from
(\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2})
to
(\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}^{\prime}\to\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}^{\prime})
lies in the essential image if and only if
for every scheme
X
, the corresponding square

\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\prime}_{1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}_{2}^{\prime}(X)}

satisfies the right (resp. left) Beck-Chevalley condition.

3.
The value of
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}
on prestacks

In this subsection we study the two ways to associate objects of
\operatorname{AGCat}
to prestacks

{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})\text{ and }{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}.

We study their interaction, questions of dualizability and behavior with respect to products.

3.1.
Sheaves on prestacks

3.1.1.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}
be a prestack locally of finite type over
B
. Recall from
[
AGKRRV1
, Appendix F]
, that the category of
\ell
-adic sheaves on
{\mathcal{Y}}

is defined by

\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\underset{S\in(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\operatorname{op}}}{\operatorname{lim}}\ \operatorname{Shv}(S)\in\operatorname{DGCat},

where the limit is taken with respect to
!
-pullbacks as transition functors.

Note that by Zariski descent, we can equivalently rewrite the above limit as

\underset{X\in(\operatorname{Sch}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\operatorname{op}}}{\operatorname{lim}}\ \operatorname{Shv}(X)\in\operatorname{DGCat}.

In particular, when
{\mathcal{Y}}=X
is a scheme, the above definition recovers the usual
\operatorname{Shv}(X)
.

Remark 3.1.2
.

Since we are working in the constructible context, we can rewrite the limit presentation of
\operatorname{Shv}(X)
as
a colimit:

\underset{X\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}}}{\operatorname{colim}}\ \operatorname{Shv}(X),

with
(-)_{!}
as transition functors, see
[
GR1
, Chapter 1, Proposition 2.5.7]
.

In particular,
\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})
is compactly generated and hence dualizable.

3.1.3.

The category
\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})
naturally upgrades to an object of
\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}
; namely, we define

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\underset{X\in(\operatorname{Sch}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\operatorname{op}}}{\operatorname{lim}}\ \underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(S)\in\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}.

It follows from Sect.
2.4.7
that for any scheme
Y
,

(3.1)

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})(X)\simeq\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}\underset{B}{\times}X).

In particular, for every
X
, the DG category
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})(X)
is compactly generated.

3.1.4.

Let
f:{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
be a morphism of prestacks over
B
. The functor between index categories

\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}}\overset{f\circ-}{\to}\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}

gives rise to a pullback functor

f^{!}:\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{2})\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1}).

In terms of (
3.1
), the above functor is given by

(f\times\operatorname{id})^{!}:\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}_{2}\underset{B}{\times}X)\to\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\underset{B}{\times}X).

The assignment
{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})
with !-pullbacks extends to a functor

(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B})^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}.

3.1.5.

Let
f:{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
is a schematic morphism of prestacks over
B
. In this case, we can define the map

f_{*}:\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1})\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{2})

given in terms of Sect.
2.4.2
by
*
-pushforward (which is defined for schematic morphisms of prestacks):

(f\times\operatorname{id}_{X})_{*}:\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\underset{B}{\times}X)\to\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}_{2}\underset{B}{\times}X).

3.1.6.

The functoriality of the
*
-pushforward functor along schematic maps can be organized as follows. Consider the 1-full symmetric monoidal category

\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{sch;all}}(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B})\subset\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B}),

where we require the
forward
morphisms to be schematic (see Sect.
2.1.3
).

Let

(3.2)

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(-):\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{sch;all}}(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B})\to\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B}

be the right Kan extension of
Equation

2.7
along the inclusion functor

\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\to\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{sch;all}}(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B}).

Note that for every prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
, the (opposite of the) functor

(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{op}})_{/{\mathcal{Y}}}\to\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch}_{/B})\underset{\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{sch;all}}(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B})}{\times}\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{sch;all}}(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B})_{{\mathcal{Y}}/}

is cofinal (since it admits a right adjoint). In particular, the restriction of
Equation

3.2
to

(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B})^{\operatorname{op}}\subset\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{sch;all}}(\operatorname{PreStk}_{/B})

agrees with that in
Section

3.1.4
.

3.2.
Cosheaves on prestacks

3.2.1.

For a prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
define

\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}:=\underset{S\in\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}}}{\operatorname{colim}}\ \operatorname{Shv}(S)\in\operatorname{DGCat},

where the colimit is taken with respect to
*
-pullshforwards as transition functors.

The equivalence (
2.12
) implies that the above colimit maps isomorphically to

\underset{X\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}}}{\operatorname{colim}}\ \operatorname{Shv}(X).

In particular, when
{\mathcal{Y}}=X
is a scheme, we recover the usual
\operatorname{Shv}(X)
.

3.2.2.

For a map of prestacks
f:{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
we have a tautologically defined functor

f_{*,\operatorname{co}}:\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1})_{\operatorname{co}}\to\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}_{2})_{\operatorname{co}}.

If
f
is schematic, we have the base change functor

\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}\to\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}},\quad S\mapsto S\underset{{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}}{\times}{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},

which gives rise to a functor

f^{!}_{\operatorname{co}}:\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}_{2})_{\operatorname{co}}\to\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}_{1})_{\operatorname{co}}.

3.2.3.

The assignment

{\mathcal{Y}}\mapsto\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}

extends to a functor

\operatorname{Shv}(-)_{\operatorname{co}}:\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{all,sch}}(\operatorname{PreStk})\to\operatorname{DGCat},

where

\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{all,sch}}(\operatorname{PreStk})\subset\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{PreStk})

is a 1-full subcategory,
in which the backward morphisms are required to be schematic.

Namely,
\operatorname{Shv}(-)_{\operatorname{co}}

is the left Kan extension of

\operatorname{Shv}(-):\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch})\to\operatorname{DGCat}

along

\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{all,sch}}(\operatorname{PreStk}).

Since the functor

\operatorname{Sch}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}}\to\operatorname{Corr}(\operatorname{Sch})\underset{\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{all,sch}}(\operatorname{PreStk})}{\times}(\operatorname{Corr}_{\operatorname{all,sch}}(\operatorname{PreStk}))_{/{\mathcal{Y}}}

is cofinal (it admits a left adjoint), the value of the above left Kan extension on
{\mathcal{Y}}
recovers
\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}
.

3.2.4.

For a prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
over
B
, set

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}:=\underset{X\in\operatorname{Sch}_{/{\mathcal{Y}}}}{\operatorname{colim}}\ \underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X)\in\operatorname{AGCat}_{/B},

where the colimit is taken with respect to the *-pushforward functors.

For
{\mathcal{Y}}=X\in\operatorname{Sch}
, we recover the usual object
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X)
.

3.2.5.

We claim:

Lemma 3.2.6
.

The value
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}(X)
of
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\operatorname{co}}
on
X
is given by

\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}\underset{B}{\times}X)_{\operatorname{co}}
.

Proof.

By Sect.
1.2.2
, we have to establish an isomorphism
